Transducing Language Models
Vésteinn Snæbjarnarson Samuel Kiegeland Tianyu Liu, Reda Boumasmoud Ryan Cotterell Tim Vieira

Abstract
Modern language models define distributions over strings, but downstream tasks often require different output formats. For instance, a model that generates byte-pair strings does not directly produce word-level predictions, and a DNA model does not directly produce amino-acid sequences. In such cases, a deterministic string-to-string transformation can convert the model’s output to the desired form. This is a familiar pattern in probability theory: applying a function f f to a random variable X ∼ p X\sim p yields a transformed random variable f ⁡ ( X ) f(X) with an induced distribution. While such transformations are occasionally used in language modeling, prior work does not treat them as yielding new, fully functional language models. We formalize this perspective and introduce a general framework for language models derived from deterministic string-to-string transformations. We focus on transformations representable as finite-state transducers—a commonly used state-machine abstraction for efficient string-to-string mappings. We develop algorithms that compose a language model with an FST to marginalize over source strings mapping to a given target, propagating probabilities through the transducer without altering model parameters and enabling conditioning on transformed outputs. We present an exact algorithm, an efficient approximation, and a theoretical analysis. We conduct experiments in three domains: converting language models from tokens to bytes, from tokens to words, and from DNA to amino acids. These experiments demonstrate inference-time adaptation of pretrained language models to match application-specific output requirements. https://github.com/rycolab/transducing-language-models



1
Introduction

Language models (LMs) define distributions over strings. Yet, the strings they produce often do not match the requirements of downstream applications, so practitioners resort to ad hoc post-processing. We call this the
string mismatch problem
.
For example, in natural language processing, modern language models typically generate byte-pair encoded strings
(
Sennrich et al. 2016
)
, while downstream tasks may require words or characters instead (see
Section

1
, below).
Similarly, DNA language models generate nucleobase sequences, whereas many applications require amino acid sequences (
Section

1
).

Adding a string-to-string transformation to a generation pipeline is a common engineering solution, such as normalizing output or mapping subword tokens to bytes.
Formally, this defines a new language model over
transformed
strings.
However, while sampling remains straightforward, other operations—such as computing the probability of a transformed string or conditioning on transformed outputs—become intractable.
Consider, for instance, the mapping from a string in any casing to its lowercase version, as in the use-case depicted in
Figure

1
. While lowercasing a given input is trivial, converting the original distribution to a distribution over lowercased words is not.

This work treats string-to-string transformations as a first-class component of the language modeling pipeline.
We show how to equip these transformed models with the familiar autoregressive interface—incremental next-symbol distributions and prefix probabilities—making them interoperable with any system built for standard autoregressive language models.
This approach to the string mismatch problem is principled, modular, and can be substantially cheaper than retraining.
Moreover, the transformations often guarantee adherence to the requirements of the downstream applications.

Transforming a language model is generally non-trivial. Even transformations with simple colloquial descriptions can be hard to apply.
The conversion to bytes (
Section

1
) can be performed using a lookup table that specifies the sequence of bytes a subword token maps to.
Similarly, the conversion from DNA to protein sequences (
Section

1
) relies on mappings from three-letter sequences of DNA bases to a single amino acid.
The grammatical word segmentation (
Section

1
) cannot be described as easily; it requires
lookahead
to determine whether, e.g., punctuation should stand alone, if it is part of the ‘
Dr.
’ title, or a decimal number ‘
3.50
’.
Simple rules alone do not ensure exact conversions.
Despite their simplicity, the byte and amino-acid transformations are not straightforward: the number of token sequences that map to each output sequence grows exponentially over the length of the output, and a proper language model transformation must account for all source-sequence probabilities.
2
2

2

This can also be seen in
Figure

1
, where an exponentially increasing number of input prefixes map to a given output prefix: with each additional symbol on the output, the number of source sequences doubles.

The decimal and title examples highlight another level of complexity, where the transformation rules require careful analysis of the surrounding context.
Recent papers have thus focused on practical methods for specific subsets of these transformations, in particular for estimating byte-level probabilities from subword models
(
Phan et al. 2024
;
Vieira et al. 2025a
;
Hayase et al. 2025
)
.
3
3

3

Our approach is most similar to that of
Vieira et al. 2025a
, which considers the case of
strict-prefix monotone transformations
(
Section

6
), which includes the subword-to-byte transformation.

We generalize these approaches to handle all of the examples given above.

This work introduces a framework for string-to-string conversion using
finite-state transducers
(FSTs). FSTs encode a powerful yet tractable family of relations, including those mentioned above. We compose pretrained language models with transducers that encode such transformations, and refer to the compositions as
transduced language models
. FSTs provide explicit structure for tracing how probabilities from the original model should map to output sequences.
This allows us to develop exact and approximate algorithms for efficient sampling, scoring, and conditioning on transformed strings, all without modifying the underlying language model.
We give sufficient conditions for when the transformations can be made exactly (
Section

6
) and approximations when exact transformations are infeasible (
Section

5
).

To validate our approach, we construct FSTs for the three use cases above: (i) converting tokens to bytes, (ii) inserting orthographic boundaries following the Penn Treebank tokenizer, and (iii) converting DNA sequences to sequences over amino acids. We then employ commonly used pretrained language models over the input units of the FSTs, and compose them with the FSTs to obtain language models over the output tokens. Finally, we use these settings to benchmark the theoretical and algorithmic contributions. In particular, we find that using a practical approximation is sufficient to obtain a good estimate at a fraction of the computational cost.

Background
4
4

4

Appendix

A
provides a notation glossary.

Strings.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
be an
alphabet
(i.e., a finite, non-empty set). Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
denote the set of all finite strings over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
, including the empty string
\varepsilon_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
.
When there is no risk of ambiguity with other alphabets, we simply write
\varepsilon
.
We use
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
to denote strings and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z^{\prime}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
to denote sets of strings.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
denote
concatenation
. Similarly, we define the concatenation of sets of strings as
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z^{\prime}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z^{\prime}}\}
, and in the singleton case as
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z^{\prime}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
when
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is a
prefix
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
when it is a
strict-prefix
. Conversely, if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
, we say
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
is an
extension
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
(a
strict extension
when
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
).

Language models.

A
language model

p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
is a probability distribution over a set of strings
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
.
Let
\textsc{eos}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
be a special
end-of-string symbol
.
We define the
prefix probability
of
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
as the probability that a string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
starts with a given prefix
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
,
and the
conditional prefix probability
:

\displaystyle{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}})\qquad\qquad{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\frac{{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}})}{{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})}\qquad\qquad{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\textsc{eos}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})}{{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})}

(1)

when
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})>0
; otherwise we set
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}0
and
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\textsc{eos}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}1
.
Therefore,
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
is a probability distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\sqcup\{\textsc{eos}\}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
.
Using this structure, any language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
may be factorized as
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})={\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\textsc{eos}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\prod_{t=1}^{\left|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\right|}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<t}\right)
.
This factorization defines a left-to-right generative process: starting from
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}=\varepsilon
, we repeatedly sample
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}\sim{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
; if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}=\textsc{eos}
, we stop, otherwise we update
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}
. Conditional generation simply starts from the conditioning prefix instead of the empty string. We refer to the quantities in
Equation

1
as the
autoregressive interface
to the language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
.

Cylindrical sets.

A cylindrical set is the set of all strings with a given prefix.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z^{\prime}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
; we define the
cylinder
over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}
as
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\rangle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
. We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}
is
cylindrical
if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\rangle
.
The union of cylinder sets is again a cylinder set, since cylinders are upward-closed.
We define the
basic cylinder
for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
as
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\langle\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\}\rangle
.
The
prefix-base
operation
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})
is defined by
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\colon\nexists\,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\}
; this operation uniquely partitions
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\rangle
into basic cylinders over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})
.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}
is
prefix-free
if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})
is prefix-free, the basic cylinders
\{\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})\}
are pairwise disjoint, and
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z}\rangle=\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Z})}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle
.

Transducers.

A
transducer
is a state-machine that encodes string-to-string relations
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\times{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
. When we express a relationship defined by
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
as a transducer, we expose the computational structure needed to develop efficient algorithms.
Formally, a
finite-state transducer
6
6

6

We refer to
Pin 2021
for a detailed treatment of transducers.
(
FST
)
f
is a tuple
(\mathsf{S},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T})
where
\mathsf{S}
is a finite set of
states
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
are alphabets of
input
and
output
symbols, respectively.
The sets
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{F}\subseteq\mathsf{S}
are the
initial
and
accepting
states.

\mathsf{T}\subseteq\mathsf{S}\times({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times\mathsf{S}
is a set of
transitions
.
We render transitions
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y},s^{\prime})\in\mathsf{T}
as
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime}
;
we say the transition
scans

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
and
emits

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
.
We write
\mathsf{T}(s)
for the set of outgoing transitions from state
s
, and
\mathsf{T}(s,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})
for those that scan
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
.
7
7

7

I.e.,
\mathsf{T}(s^{\prime\prime})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime})\in\mathsf{T}\colon s=s^{\prime\prime}\}
, and
\mathsf{T}(s^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime\prime}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime})\in\mathsf{T}\colon s=s^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime\prime}}\}
.

The transducer
f
defines a set of
paths

\Pi
. Each
path

\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi
is a sequence of transitions of the form
s_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}}s_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{2}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{2}}s_{2}\cdots s_{N-1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{N}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}}s_{N}
.
We call
\boldsymbol{\pi}
an
accepting path
if
s_{0}\in\mathsf{I}
and
s_{N}\in\mathsf{F}
.
The
relation defined
by
f
is given by
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\rrbracket\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mid s_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}}s_{1}\cdots s_{N-1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{N}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}}s_{N}\in\Pi\colon s_{0}\in\mathsf{I},s_{N}\in\mathsf{F}\}
, i.e., each accepting path contributes (not necessarily uniquely) a pair of scanned and emitted strings.
When every transition of a transducer scans and emits the same symbol (
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
), the machine acts as a
finite-state automaton
(
acceptor
) that recognizes a
language

L\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
—the set of strings admitted by at least one accepting path.
Such a machine is a
nondeterministic finite automaton
(
NFA
) in general; it is a

deterministic finite automaton
(
DFA
) if it has a single initial state, no
\varepsilon
-transitions, and at most one transition per state and input symbol.
Every NFA can be converted to an equivalent DFA by
determinization

(
Rabin & Scott 1959
)
.
For completeness,
Appendix

B
provides additional background.

3
Transduced Language Models

A
transduced language model

p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}
arises from applying a string-to-string
transformation

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
, encoded by a transducer
f
, to a string drawn from a
source language model

p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
. Formally, if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X})
has the following probability mass function:

p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X})\right]=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(2)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is the
preimage
of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\}
.
Put differently, in
Equation

2
, we sum over the strings
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
Unfortunately, evaluating
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
exactly using
Equation

2
is generally infeasible (since the preimage
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
can be very large), even though exact sampling from
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}
is efficient (by sampling
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
and applying
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
).
Like all language models, a transduced language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}
has prefix and conditional prefix probability functions; its prefix probability is

\displaystyle{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}X})\right]=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(3)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is the
precover
of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
, defined as
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\}
.
8
8

8

Note that the precover depends on
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
; we suppress this dependency when it is clear from context.

Prefix probabilities yield a conditional factorization of string probability (see
Section

2
), enabling efficient left-to-right autoregressive generation.
We develop a method in
Section

4
that allows us to compute the sum in
Equation

3
in
finite
time for a general class of mappings, such as those mentioned in the introduction (i.e., normalizing text, inserting orthographic word boundaries, or converting DNA to amino-acid sequences). In
Section

5
, we present algorithms to compute these quantities.

4
Decomposing the Precover

In
Section

3
, we saw that if we can sum over the precover of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, we can calculate
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})

(
Equation

3
), unlocking an autoregressive interface
to the transduced language model.
The following two examples illustrate how we can often compute this infinite sum by exploiting structural properties of the transducer.

Example 1
.

The transducer below lowercases a string.
For the target string
ab
, the precover is the infinite set
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{ab}})=\langle\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{AB}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Ab}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aB}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ab}}\}\rangle
.
Given a model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
over the input language, the derivation on the right (
4a
–
4c
) applies
Equation

3
to express
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{ab}})
as a sum of four source-language prefix probabilities:

0
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{A}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{B}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}}

\vdots

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Z}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{z}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{b}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}}

\vdots

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{z}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{z}}

\displaystyle{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{ab}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{ab}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(4a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{AB}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Ab}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aB}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ab}}\}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\rangle}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(4b)

\displaystyle={\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{AB}})+{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Ab}})+{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aB}})+{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ab}})

(4c)

In
1
, each input symbol maps to exactly one output symbol, so the precover decomposes neatly into cylinders. The next example shows what happens when some source strings cover the target string, while their extensions do not.

Example 2
.

.
The derivation on the right (

(5c)

Because
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{bad}}\in\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ba}}\rangle
but
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{bad}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{ba}})
, the precover cannot be decomposed entirely into cylinders.
Instead, we decompose it into two disjoint parts: a maximal cylindrical subset and its complement in the precover.
The
quotient
collects the shortest element of each cylinder; the complement is the
remainder
.
The final step (
5c
) illustrates a
computational shortcut
; for any
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
we can decompose
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
:

{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\underbrace{\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}}_{\text{Quotient}}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})+\underbrace{\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}}_{\text{Remainder}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(6)

and marks the cylinder over the quotient.

We now formalize the remainder, quotient, and decomposition for any string-to-string function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
.

The prefix decomposition of the precover.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
be a map. For each
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
, define

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\colon\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\}
; this set is a cylinder—if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
then every extension of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
also belongs to
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
—making it the largest cylinder contained in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
We define the
quotient
and
remainder
of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\operatorname{pf}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})

(7)

We call the pair
\left({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}),\ {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\right)
the
optimal prefix decomposition
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, characterized by three conditions:
(i)
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is
prefix-free
,
(ii)
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(
validity
), and
(iii)
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(
maximality
)—
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
identifies the largest cylinder in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
This decomposition (indeed, any valid one) lets us compute prefix probabilities using the shortcut:

\displaystyle{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\,\sqcup\,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\end{subarray}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}})+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(8a)

Section

5
provides an algorithm for computing the prefix decomposition;
Section

6
identifies when it is finite.

5
Algorithms

We now present an algorithm for computing prefix decompositions by exploiting the explicit structure of a transducer that encodes the function.
Combined with the computational shortcut (
Equation

6
), this gives an autoregressive interface to transduced language models.
We first describe the algorithm abstractly, then instantiate its checks using a transducer, and finally discuss optimizations.

Figure 2:
Decomposition algorithm.

Figure

2
gives the decomposition algorithm (
decompose
), which maintains a queue of candidate source strings and explores them by breadth-first search (BFS), optionally pruning low-probability candidates at each step (described below).
11
11

11

For efficiency,
decompose
should be memoized. For
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|>0
, the recursive call
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}_{<N})
seeds the queue with the previous decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}^{\prime}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}^{\prime}
rather than enumerating from
\varepsilon
.

Each dequeued string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
undergoes three checks, defined in terms of the precover
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
:

1.

Cylinder
:
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_cylinder}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\!\Longleftrightarrow\!\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, i.e., every extension of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
covers
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is added to the quotient set
Q
and not explored further (
).

2.

Member
:
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_member}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\!\Longleftrightarrow\!{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
itself covers
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
When
is_cylinder
is false but
is_member
is true,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is added to the remainder set
R
; its extensions are still explored (
).

3.

Live
:
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_live}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\!\Longleftrightarrow\!\exists{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, i.e., some extension of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
belongs to the precover.
Only live extensions are enqueued (
).

Because strings are processed shortest-first, if any prefix of the current string had already entered the quotient, the current string would never have been enqueued.
Once the queue is exhausted, the algorithm returns the prefix decomposition.
We instantiate these checks concretely in
Section

5.1
.

Theorem 5.1

(Correctness of
decompose
)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
admits a finite decomposition and the three checks exactly implement the conditions above with no pruning, then
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
terminates and its output
({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}})
is the optimal prefix decomposition (
Equation

7
).

We verify the three conditions of the optimal prefix decomposition (
Equation

7
).

1.

Prefix-freeness
(
Q
is prefix-free):
When
is_cylinder
succeeds for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
(
), the
continue
(
) skips the extension loop, so no
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}
is enqueued.
Since strings enter the queue only as single-symbol extensions of dequeued strings, no extension of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is ever enqueued or dequeued, and thus no two elements of
Q
share a prefix.

2.

Validity
(
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}
):
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
; we first show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}
.
We show by induction on prefix length that either a prefix of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
enters
Q
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
itself is dequeued and added to
Q
or
R
.
The base case holds:
\varepsilon
is enqueued.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<k}
is dequeued and not placed in
Q
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<k+1}
is enqueued by the extension loop: since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<k+1}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, we have
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_live}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<k+1},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(
).
By induction, either some prefix
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<j}
enters
Q
—giving
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<j}\rangle\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle
—or
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
itself is dequeued, the cylinder check fails, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
gives
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}
via
is_member
(
).
The reverse direction is clear since elements of
Q
pass the exact
is_cylinder
check, so
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
Similarly, elements of
R
pass
is_member
, so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
What remains is to show that
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle
and
R
are disjoint.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
was dequeued and
is_cylinder
failed, so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}
.
No proper prefix of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is in
Q
either—otherwise
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
would never have been enqueued (prefix-freeness).
Hence
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\notin\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle
.

3.

Maximality
(
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
):
shortest-first processing ensures no proper prefix of a quotient element satisfies
is_cylinder
(
), so
Q
is the
minimal
prefix-free set of cylinders.
Together with the exact cylinder check, the earliest qualifying prefix is always found, as required by
Equation

7
.
It remains to show
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle
. By validity,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}
. Every element of
R
fails
is_cylinder
, so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\emptyset
. Hence
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle
.

Termination
:
is_live
(
) ensures only viable extensions are enqueued; finiteness of the decomposition guarantees the queue empties.
∎

Conservative checks and suboptimality.

If the checks used by
decompose
are conservative approximations, the algorithm may produce
suboptimal
decompositions—valid but with a smaller quotient than the optimal one.
A conservative liveness check (false positives) enqueues unnecessary extensions but does not affect classification: every dequeued string is still correctly classified by
is_cylinder
and
is_member
, so the result remains optimal.
A conservative cylinder check (false negatives) may fail to recognize some quotient elements, placing them in
R
instead.
The decomposition remains valid, but because a successful cylinder check terminates exploration of that subtree (
), missing a cylinder means the BFS continues exploring extensions that would otherwise have been cut off, potentially inflating the remainder and increasing the computation.
By contrast, the membership check must be exact to preserve validity.

Approximation via pruning.

When the prefix decomposition becomes large, exhaustively enumerating and scoring can become infeasible.
In these cases, we use a pruning strategy (
prune
) that sorts candidates by prefix probability and removes those whose cumulative probability mass falls below a specified threshold
\tau
.
This discards low-probability candidates to keep decomposition tractable.
Since pruning only removes candidates from the queue, every element found is correct—
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rangle\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
—but the decomposition is no longer valid in general (coverage may be incomplete), so the computed prefix probability is a lower bound on the true value.
Our strategy is detailed in
Section

C.3
.

counterexamples

Figure 3:
State-based instantiation of the checks from
Figure

2
on the precover DFA
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. Helper functions
step
and
run
advance through the DFA;
is_member
and
is_live
are single-state lookups;
is_cylinder
performs a BFS to verify universality.

We now show how to instantiate the three checks (
Figure

2
) using a finite-state transducer. This serves as a pedagogical introduction;
Appendix

C
describes a more detailed, but faster algorithm.

We represent the transformation
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
with a transducer
f
(see
Sections

2
and
B
).
Given a target prefix
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})
is an NFA that accepts exactly
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
To enable the efficient state-based checks below, we determinize and trim this NFA to obtain a DFA:

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\mathsf{trim}(\mathsf{determinize}(\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})))

Let
\mathsf{S}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
\mathsf{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
\mathsf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, and
\mathsf{T}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
denote the components of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
is deterministic, scanning a source string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{N}
yields a unique state, which we denote by
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
.
We now describe how to implement the three checks using
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
(pseudocode in
Figure

3
).

•

Cylinder
:
We need to check whether
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
to decide if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
. Let
S=\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
be the unique state reached after scanning
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
is deterministic,
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
if and only if
S
is
universal
:
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{}_{[S]}\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, meaning every continuation of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is accepted.
The
is_cylinder
check (
Figure

3
) tests universality via breadth-first search (BFS) from
S
.

•

Member
:
When the
is_cylinder
check fails for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
with
S=\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
, we need to determine whether the scanned string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
. Here it suffices to check if
S\in\mathsf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, i.e., if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
accepts
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
.

•

Live
:
We construct
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
as a
trimmed
automaton accepting
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
.
12
12

12

This can also be expressed as
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})=\llbracket\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})\rrbracket
.

Trimming ensures that every reachable state lies on some accepting path, so liveness reduces to
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\neq\emptyset
.

Although
decompose
is written in terms of source strings, each check internally computes the state
S=\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
reached by scanning
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
in the deterministic
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, and reduces to a state property.

5.2
Optimizations

The algorithm above is correct but impractical for large transducers. In
Appendix

C
, we describe several optimizations; we summarize the key ideas here.

Lazy determinization.

Eagerly determinizing
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
is often computationally expensive. Instead, we track
frontiers
: sets of transducer states reachable after scanning a source prefix, paired with the output emitted so far. Frontiers lazily perform the subset construction
(
Rabin & Scott 1959
, the standard NFA-to-DFA conversion;)
and the composition with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
simultaneously, avoiding both eager determinization and eager composition.
The three checks—cylinder, member, and liveness—are now defined in terms of the frontier rather than a single DFA state (
Section

C.2
).

Incremental next-symbol decomposition.

To efficiently compute
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
, we introduce
decompose_next
(
Section

C.4
), which derives the decomposition of each extension
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}
from the decomposition of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
It operates in a single BFS pass that handles each
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
simultaneously.

Decomposition shortcuts.

Many structural properties of the decomposition allow the BFS to skip work: non-cylinder monotonicity (
Proposition

C.3
), cylinder uniqueness (
Proposition

C.4
), input-projection universality (
Section

C.6
), combined universality (
Proposition

C.5
), and an all-universal fast path (
Figure

14
).

Lazier enumeration.

Rather than explicitly composing the transducer with the copy transducer
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
, the frontier-based algorithms track output buffers directly, performing the composition lazily and avoiding the materialization cost.
Additionally, we precompute states whose input projection accepts all of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
(
Section

C.6
).
When a frontier reaches such a state with a buffer that already covers
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, the cylinder check succeeds immediately, bypassing the expensive check.

Label pushing.

As a standard preprocessing step
(
Oncina et al. 1993
;
Mohri 2003
;
Mohri 2009
, see, e.g.,)
, we push output labels toward the initial state of
f
: when every path through a state
s
emits output beginning with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
, that
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
is shifted to the incoming arc so it is produced one step earlier.
This ensures that output is committed as early as possible, speeding up the frontier-based cylinder check.

6
Sufficient Conditions for Finite Decompositions

The decomposition algorithms in
Section

5
enumerate strings by breadth-first search.
For the enumeration to terminate, the quotient
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and remainder
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
need to be finite for a given target string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
.
Previous work (
Vieira et al. 2025a
, Props 1 and 3; also in
Section

D.2
) showed that strict-prefix monotonicity guarantees an empty remainder and a bounded quotient.
This ensures that
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is computable.

For functions that are not strict-prefix monotone, however, the decomposition may be infinite.
In this section, we give additional conditions for finite decomposition.
We first give function-level properties that guarantee an empty remainder and bounded quotient, and then transducer-level conditions for when the quotient and remainder are finite.

We say that a map
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
is
strict-prefix monotone
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\prec{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\prec{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})
.
Similarly
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is
prefix monotone
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})
.
We say that a map
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is
prefix-continuous
if, for every
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
, the set
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is cylindrical, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\emptyset
.
The following proposition shows that prefix monotonicity implies prefix-continuity:

Proposition 6.1
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is prefix monotone

(ii)

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle)\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}

(iii)

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}

(iv)

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is prefix-continuous.

[Proof:
Section

D.1
]

6.1
shows how we generalize
Vieira et al. 2025a
.
First, by relaxing strict-prefix monotonicity to prefix monotonicity, we support multi-symbol lookahead in the quotient.
Section

1
is an example of this: it is prefix monotone and requires two-symbol lookahead before committing to an output. Second, by introducing the remainder, we can handle functions that are not prefix monotone, meaning that there can be source strings that cover the target, but not all of their extensions do (e.g.,
2
).

6.1
gives sufficient conditions on a transducer that guarantee a finite decomposition for every target string, even when the underlying function is not prefix monotone.
The key notion is
safety
: a state is safe if it is IP-universal, has
finite closure
(i.e.,
|\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]}\rrbracket|<\infty
), or all its successors are safe.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
be a function realized by a transducer
f
.
The decomposition
({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}),{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}))
is finite for every
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
if:

(i)

No
\varepsilon
-output cycles:

f
contains no cycle in which every arc outputs
\varepsilon
.

(ii)

Safety:
Every state of
f
is
safe
, defined inductively as the smallest set such that
s
is safe if:
(a)
s
is IP-universal;
(b)
|\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]}\rrbracket|<\infty
(finite closure); or
(c) for all transitions
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime}
is safe.

Proof: See
Section

D.3
.

The conditions in
6.1
guarantee exact computation.
In particular, these are satisfied by the transducers introduced in the experiments section (
Section

7
): the token-to-byte transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
and the DNA-to-amino-acid transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{dna2aa}}
, whose quotients are finite and remainders empty, but not by the PTB transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
, whose quotients are infinite.

The finiteness of decomposition is a property of the function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
, not of any particular transducer encoding it. The lemma’s conditions are sufficient but not necessary: safety tests each state individually, whereas the decomposition algorithm tests universality of frontiers (sets of state/string pairs), which can succeed even when individual states are not safe.
There are many cases where the prefix decomposition is infinite, or otherwise prohibitively large; in these cases, we let the source language model determine which prefix decomposition members carry the most significant mass and prune others (
Section

5
). This lets us greedily approximate the probabilities of the transduced language model by enumerating a high-mass subset of the decomposition.

7
Experiments

We now consider three examples of transduced language models. For each use case, we follow the approach in
Vieira et al. 2025a
and measure the Jensen–Shannon divergence (JSD) between the distributions obtained using the approximation via probability mass pruning mentioned in
Section

C.3
and a reference distribution we get by choosing a pruning threshold
\tau
. We also report cross-entropy loss (
Section

G.4
), reflecting the cost of scoring specific sequences rather than full distributions. Experiments use GPT-2 Large (
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}
)
(
Radford et al. 2019
)
, LLaMA 3.2-1B (
p_{\mathsf{llama1B}}
) and LLaMA 3.1-8B (
p_{\mathsf{llama8B}}
)
(
Team 2024
)
, Phi-4 (
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}
)
(
Abdin et al. 2024
, 14B;)
, and a DNA model trained on the human genome (
p_{\mathsf{dna}}
).
13
13

13

Links to models:

https://huggingface.co/openai-community/gpt2-large
,

https://huggingface.co/meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B
,

https://huggingface.co/meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B
,

https://huggingface.co/microsoft/phi-4

and
https://huggingface.co/vesteinn/gpt2-dna
.

For the experiments in
Section

7
, we use
genlm-bytes
14
14

14

genlm-bytes
is an implementation of
Vieira et al. 2025a
; see
https://github.com/genlm/genlm-bytes
.
to convert token-level models to byte-level models. See
Appendix

F
for details on the training and evaluation setup, and
Section

F.5
for transducer details.

Recall that a transduced language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}
is characterized by a source model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
and a transducer
f
encoding some function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
(
Section

3
). To make this dependency explicit, we write
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}
and refer to the operation as composing. All experiments compute next-symbol distributions using the more efficient algorithm (
Section

C.8
), which reuses cached decompositions across target positions and exploits structural shortcuts (
Section

C.5
).
Probability mass pruning (
Section

C.3
) retains the most likely decomposition elements such that the discarded probability mass does not exceed the pruning threshold.

Our experiments span the prefix-monotonicity spectrum (
6.1
): token-to-byte (T2B) is strict-prefix monotone, DNA-to-amino-acid is prefix monotone with multi-symbol lookahead, and PTB tokenization is not prefix monotone.

From tokens to bytes.

We revisit the algorithm for converting models from tokens to bytes, as in
Vieira et al. 2025a
.
This transformation can be realized by a simple transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
, with
\mathcal{O}(|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}|)
states.
Specifically, the transducer contains a chain for each token present in the input vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
. This structure allows us to use a shortcut (see
Figure

14
) that resolves each quotient element with a single LM call. An example of such a machine is given in
Figure

5
.
We benchmark the algorithms in
Section

5
using the transducers
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
,
p_{\mathsf{llama1B}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
,
p_{\mathsf{llama8B}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
, and
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
on the first ten paragraphs
of the
wikitext-2-raw-v1
dataset
(
Merity et al. 2017
)
(corresponding to the first 7684 bytes). As shown in
Figure

6
(left) and
Table

8
in
Appendix

G
, lower pruning thresholds (
\tau
) give lower JSD values against a reference distribution (
\tau=
1e-5) at the cost of throughput, measured in bytes per second.
We confirm that the JSD values are similar to those achieved by
Vieira et al. 2025a
in
Section

G.1
,
Table

6
. Their method is limited to strict-prefix monotone transformations, which enables a specialized trie-based algorithm that achieves higher throughput while maintaining comparable accuracy.

From tokens to orthographic word boundaries.

The next transformation we consider is converting language models over tokens to language models over
orthographic
words.
The precise definition of what constitutes such a word varies depending on the application.
In some settings, contractions such as “
wouldn’t
” should be treated as a single unit. In other settings, however, a more natural segmentation would be “
would
” and “
n’t
”.
We can accommodate any FST-based definition.
Linguistic tokenizers, such as the PTB tokenizer
(
Marcus et al. 1993
)
, use contextual information to segment raw English text into linguistically meaningful units suitable for downstream NLP tasks.
We construct an FST that encodes the PTB tokenizer,
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
; details are in
Section

F.5
. An example of a rule in the transducer is given in
Figure

5
, which inserts
sep
before a comma if a digit does not follow.
Composing
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
yields a transduced language model, mapping subword tokens to PTB tokens. To reduce the state count of the composed FST and improve efficiency, we represent
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
using the byte-transformed models from

genlm-bytes
.
15
15

15

https://github.com/genlm/genlm-bytes
that implements
Vieira et al. 2025a
’s (
Vieira et al. 2025a
) method.

In
Figure

6
(right), we plot the average JSD against the throughput for different thresholds
\tau
, using the same dataset as in
Section

7
. The reference distribution uses
\tau=
1e-4 (3e-4 for
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}
).
As with the experiments in
Section

7
, we observe lower JSD at lower thresholds, albeit at the cost of throughput. For experiments using higher thresholds, see
Appendix

G
,
Table

9
.

Converting DNA models to models over amino acids.

We use a transducer that converts sequences of the four DNA nucleotides to sequences over the twenty-two amino acids.
16
16

16

I.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{G}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{T}}\right\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{R}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{N}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{D}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{Q}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{E}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{G}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{H}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{I}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{L}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{K}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{M}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{F}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{P}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{V}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{W}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{Y}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{B}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{Z}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{*}}\right\}
.

Let
p_{\mathsf{dna}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{dna2aa}}
denote the transduced model that converts DNA nucleotides into amino acids.
To evaluate our approach, we sample 65 human proteins.
17
17

17

Sampled from
https://www.uniprot.org/uniprotkb?query=Human
, see
Table

1
for the accession numbers.
In
Table

10
, we show the average JSD and throughput for different thresholds
\tau
. Note that this transducer is particularly challenging since the set of candidates in the decomposed precover grows exponentially with the sequence length. To mitigate the combinatorial blow-up, we cap the candidate-set and report throughput and JSD while varying the cap.

8
Conclusion

We have introduced a general framework for transforming language models using transducers. Empirically, we have shown that our beam-summing approximation efficiently transduces token-based LLMs into models over bytes, words, and even amino acids, without requiring retraining. Our theoretical analysis characterizes the conditions under which such mappings can be performed exactly. The proposed approach is an effective way to repurpose existing language models and to accurately compute probabilities for any unit and transformation defined by a transducer. By reducing the problem to transducer decomposition, the framework opens the door to leveraging future advances in finite-state methods for language model adaptation. (Future work and limitations are discussed in
Appendix

J
.)

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
