Notation conventions.

Color
encodes domain:
magenta
denotes source-domain objects (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}
) and
olive green
denotes target-domain objects (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
).

Font
encodes type: lowercase italic for symbols (
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
), bold italic for strings (
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
), calligraphic for alphabets and sets (
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}
), sans-serif for transducer components (
\mathsf{S}
,
\mathsf{T}
,
\mathsf{I}
,
\mathsf{F}
,
\mathsf{U}
), and typewriter for the transducer itself (
f
) and for concrete strings in examples (
abc
,
xyz
).
The overrightarrow
\overrightarrow{\cdot}
marks prefix probabilities:
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}
is the prefix probability of
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
.

Appendix B
Background on Transducers

This section introduces additional information on transducers, complementing
Section

2
.

Transducer variants.

We say a transducer is
functional
if it defines a function, and
partially functional
if it defines a partial function.
A transducer is
input-deterministic

if for every state
s\in\mathsf{S}
,
|\mathsf{T}(s,\varepsilon)|=0
and
|\mathsf{T}(s,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})|\leq 1
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
18
18

18

The more general notion of a
subsequential transducer
additionally includes a final output function; this distinction does not arise for copy-transducers.
.
For input-deterministic transducers, each source string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
has at most one accepting path that scans
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
and, therefore, can emit at most one target string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
.
Thus, every input-deterministic transducer defines a (partial) function.
A
copy-transducer
is one where every transition emits the same symbol as it scans, i.e., every transition is of the form
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}}s^{\prime}
.
We abbreviate copy transitions as
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}}s^{\prime}
.
19
19

19

For readers familiar with finite-state automata, every copy-transducer is isomorphic to an
acceptor
.

Copy-transducers define partial identity functions: they map each string in a designated subset to itself, and drop all others.
In the same way that we abbreviate copy transitions, we may implicitly map them to a set of strings via
({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
.

Operations.

Transducers support
composition
: given transducers
f
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}^{\prime}
, their composition
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}^{\prime}
is a transducer denoting
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}^{\prime}\rrbracket\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}^{\prime}\rrbracket
.
20
20

20

Here,
relation composition
is given by
f\circ g\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{(x,z)\mid(x,y)\in f,(y,z)\in g\}
where
f
and
g
are relations.
21
21

21

Pin 2025
gives an efficient method for constructing
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}^{\prime}
.

We denote a transducer that encodes the relationship
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\rrbracket\circ\{({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}\in\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle\}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
. We freely coerce between these representations when the intent is clear from context.
We define the
input projection
operation encoding the relationship
\llbracket\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}})\rrbracket=\{({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\mid\exists\,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\colon({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\in\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\rrbracket\}
as
\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}(\mathsf{S},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{F},\{(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}}s^{\prime})\mid s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}\})
, which is a copy-transducer.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]}
denote the operation of
force-starting

f
in state
s
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}(\mathsf{S},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}},\{s\},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T})
; this operation yields a machine defining the set of source–target suffix pairs that are generated by paths starting at a given
s
and ending in an accepting state.
We say that a state
s
is
IP-universal
(
input-projection universal
) if
\llbracket\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]})\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, i.e., no matter what input follows, the transducer can still produce output. Let
\mathsf{U}\subseteq\mathsf{S}
denote the set of IP-universal states; this set can be precomputed for each transducer (
Section

C.6
).

Our algorithms use an
input-determinization
transformation
\mathsf{determinize}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}})
that maps a (partially) functional transducer
f
to an equivalent one that is input-deterministic. In general, such a mapping is not always realizable with a finite number of states.
22
22

22

Choffrut 1977
gives such an algorithm, by using a power set construction on the input side as in the determinization of nondeterministic finite automata. He shows it yields a finite machine if and only if the automaton has
bounded variation
, the constraint that for any two strings whose prefix distance (the combined length of the strings with the longest shared prefix removed) is bounded, then the output prefix distance is also bounded. He also provides a testable condition for determinization, known as the twinning condition: once two runs have read the same input prefix (i.e., we cut the input at the same point), then for every common continuation, they append the same further output.

However, in the special case of copy-transducers, input-determinization is always possible, but may result in exponential blowup in the worst case.
23
23

23

For readers familiar with finite-state automata, input-determinization of a copy-transducer is isomorphic to the determinization of an equivalent finite-state automaton, see
Footnote

19
.

We use
\mathsf{trim}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}})
to denote a
trimming
operation that removes all states and edges that do not appear on any accepting path.
These are standard operations that are implemented in any FST library; more details can be found in
(
Pin 2021
;
Pin 2025
)
.

Visual notation.

We use diagrams like those shown in
Appendix

E
to represent transducers. Transitions without source states denote initial states; double-lined states indicate accepting states. Transitions
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime}
are shown as arrows between states.

Limitations.

Finite-state transducers define the class of
rational relations

(
Berstel 1979
, Ch. III)
. Because FSTs only have finitely many states, they are inherently limited in the relations they can represent. For example, FSTs cannot perform transformations that require unbounded matching or counting. In contrast, transducers with unbounded memory extend beyond the rational class, offering greater expressive power, but come with increased complexity and often undecidability of key properties, such as
universality
.

Appendix C
Efficient Algorithms

The decomposition algorithm of
Section

5
operates on a determinized, trimmed precover machine
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}=\mathsf{trim}(\mathsf{determinize}(\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})))
. In practice, both the composition with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
and the explicit determinization are expensive and must be repeated for each target
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
The algorithms in this section perform both steps lazily, operating directly on the original transducer
f
and avoiding explicit materialization.
This requires careful bookkeeping of partial outputs via
frontiers
(
Section

C.1.1
), but enables precomputation of IP-universal states and incremental reuse across targets.

We begin by stating the algorithmic goal (
Section

C.1
), then introduce the frontier data structure (
Section

C.1.1
) and show how each check reduces to a frontier query (
Section

C.2
).
We write
({\mathsf{S},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{T}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}
throughout and omit the dependency on
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}
since it is clear from context.

C.1
The Goal: An Efficient Implementation of the Autoregressive Interface

We seek efficient algorithms for the prefix probabilities
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, string probabilities
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, and next-token distributions
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
of transduced language models. Specifically, we want efficient implementations of the following methods, which constitute the interface to the transduced language model (
Section

2
).

The primitive operation above is
prefix_prob
, which requires a single call to
decompose
. Both
next_dist
and
prob
are derived from it:
next_dist
computes
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}|
additional prefix probabilities and obtains
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}(\textsc{eos}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
by complement;
prob
is a one-line product. In an implementation,
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}
and
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
should be memoized so that extending
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
to
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})
requires only a single conditional evaluation rather than replaying the entire history (see
Section

C.7
).
In
Section

C.4
, we show how to compute
next_dist
with a more efficient, joint decomposition, rather than
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}|{+}1
separate calls to
prefix_prob
.

The three checks in
Figure

2
—
is_cylinder
,
is_member
, and
is_live
—all require determining which transducer states are reachable after reading a source string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
, and what output each has produced relative to
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. We capture this information in a single data structure: the
frontier
(
Section

C.1.1
).
In
Section

C.2
, we show how each check reduces to a simple query on the frontier.
In practice, the quotient and remainder may be large or infinite, so we introduce pruning (
Section

C.3
) to obtain practical approximations.

C.1.1
Frontier Computation

Since
f
can be nondeterministic even if it is functional, the transducer
f
may reach many states simultaneously for a given source string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
and target
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. Each of these paths may have produced a different output buffer.
The
frontier

\mathcal{F}=\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
collects this information: it is the set of
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
pairs—where
s\in\mathsf{S}
is a transducer state and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
is the output produced so far—reachable by reading
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
from the initial states, filtered to buffers
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}
compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
24
24

24

A buffer
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}
is compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}
, i.e., the buffer has either covered the target or has yet to diverge from it.
The frontier encodes all information needed for the three checks:
is_cylinder
,
is_member
, and
is_live
can each be evaluated from
\mathcal{F}
alone, without retaining the full path history (
Section

C.2
;
Figure

8
).
The pseudocode in
Figure

7
describes how to compute the frontier using the three functions
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}step}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, and
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}closure}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.

Figure 7:
Frontier-based state machine

The frontier replaces the explicit precover machine
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}=\mathsf{trim}(\mathsf{determinize}(\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})))
used in
Section

5
.
Rather than materializing
P
, the frontier tracks the same information directly on
f
, avoiding both the composition with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
and the explicit determinization.
The design reflects a two-phase structure: before a path’s buffer covers the target, the frontier tracks per-state output buffers
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
filtered by target compatibility; once the buffer reaches or passes
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, the frontier can be truncated for universality checking (
Section

C.2
).
The frontier unifies the two phases into a single lazy data structure.

Figure 8:
Truncated frontier example for the target
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}
.

Top:
the transducer
f
.

Middle:
the frontier graph
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}}}
, whose nodes are sets of
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
pairs and whose edges are input symbols.
Buffers are truncated to length
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|{+}1=2
: advancing
(q_{3},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{cd}})
by any input produces
cdd
, truncated back to
cd
, yielding the self-loop.
Truncation keeps the state space finite while preserving whether the buffer has reached the target and which next output symbol it commits to.
Reading
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}
reaches two states via
\varepsilon
-closure:
q_{1}
(buffer
={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
) and
q_{3}
(buffer
\succ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
).

Yellow
nodes are universal—every input leads to a non-empty accepting successor—placing
a
in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}})
.
The
teal
node
q_{2}
has no arc on
b
, so the BFS fails and
b
enters
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}})
.

The frontier enjoys a useful monotonicity property: extending the target by one symbol can only remove elements from the frontier, never add new ones.
This means we can compute the frontier incrementally—filtering rather than rebuilding—as the target grows.

Proposition C.1

(Frontier containment)
.

For any source string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
, target string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
with
N>0
:

\displaystyle\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\{(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\colon|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}|<N\lor{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}_{N}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}\}

(9)

Buffer filtering in
step
and
closure
keeps only
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
pairs where
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}
and the target agree on their shared prefix.
Since FST arcs only append to the buffer, once
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}
is long enough to have an
N\textsuperscript{th}
symbol and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}_{N}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
, then all descendant buffers would be filtered out by the
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
-compatibility filter.
Therefore, filtering
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
at the end is equivalent
to filtering at each step.

To see this, note that the
(\supseteq)
direction holds because
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
-compatible elements are also
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N-1}
-compatible (the former is strictly more restrictive).
The
(\subseteq)
direction holds because elements that are
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N-1}
-compatible but
not

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
-compatible (i.e.,
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}|\geq N
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}_{N}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
) cannot produce descendants that become
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
-compatible, since buffers only grow.
∎

Proposition C.2
.

Proposition

C.1
generalizes beyond single-symbol extensions: for any
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
:

\displaystyle\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\{(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\}

(10)

By induction on
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|-|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}|
.
The base case
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}
is immediate.
For the inductive step, write
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}^{\prime}
.
By
Proposition

C.1
,

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\{(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\colon|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}|<N\lor{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}_{N}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}\}
.
By the induction hypothesis,

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})=\{(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}^{\prime}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}^{\prime}\}
.
Substituting,
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
survives both filters iff
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}
agrees with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
on all positions up to
\min(|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}|,N)
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.∎
Proposition

C.2
is the key property that enables warm-starting
decompose_next
(
Section

C.4
) from any target prefix’s cached frontiers.

C.2
Frontier-Based Checks (
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_cylinder}}},\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_member}}},\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_live}}}
)

Figure 9:
Frontier-based checks.

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_cylinder}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
verifies universality via powerset BFS over projected frontiers.

is_member
checks for an accepting covering state;
is_live
checks whether the frontier is non-empty.

To check whether
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, the frontier-based
is_cylinder
applies
trunc_buf
to the frontier
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
, which truncates buffers to length
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|
, yielding a
truncated frontier
—a set of
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
pairs whose buffers are compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
It then verifies universality via BFS over truncated frontiers: each successor frontier is obtained by applying
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}step}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
followed by
trunc_buf
.
Unlike the ordinary frontier, where buffers can grow without bound, truncated buffers must be prefixes of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, so at most
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|{+}1
distinct buffers exist, making the truncated frontier state space finite.
Each truncated frontier corresponds to a state of the determinized precover machine
P
, so the BFS terminates.
The BFS checks that every truncated frontier is both accepting (some pair has
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
and
s\in\mathsf{F}
) and complete (every input symbol leads to a non-empty successor).
Without this exploration, two nondeterministic paths may yield the same string yet end in different states
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, where neither is universal on its own, although
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s_{1}]}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s_{2}]}\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
.
25
25

25

Universality is decidable for finite-state automata; efficient algorithms include antichain-based simulation
(
De Wulf et al. 2006
)
, bisimulation up to congruence
(
Bonchi & Pous 2015
)
, and classical equivalence checking
(
Meyer & Stockmeyer 1972
)
.

The precomputation of IP-universal states and a cylinder check shortcut are discussed in
Section

C.6
.

When the cylinder check fails, membership reduces to checking whether the frontier contains a covering accepting pair (
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
and
s\in\mathsf{F}
): if so,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
enters
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.

Liveness is non-emptiness of the frontier. Since frontier pairs are already filtered to target-compatible buffers, a non-empty frontier witnesses that some extension of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
can reach the precover.

C.3
Pruning (
prune
)

Even when the decomposition is finite, the quotient and remainder can be very large. Enumerating all of them is often impractical, so pruning is essential for scalability.
26
26

26

An alternative to deterministic pruning is importance sampling with a proposal distribution over source strings; we leave this direction to future work.

At minimum,
prune
drops source strings with zero prefix probability, since these cannot contribute any mass:

⬇

def

@$
\

(\

)
$@

@$

\

\

\

\

)

$@

This simplistic filter drops only strings with
zero
probability. In practice, we use a probability-mass pruning strategy: given candidates
\texttt{q}^{\prime}
with total mass
Z=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in\texttt{q}^{\prime}}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
, we sort by descending
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}
and greedily retain candidates until their cumulative mass reaches
(1-\tau)\cdot Z
or the capacity
n_{\texttt{max}}
is reached, whichever comes first.
The resulting decomposition is approximate: discarded candidates contribute at most
\tau\cdot Z
mass per step, but the error vanishes as
\tau\to 0
.

Approximation bound.

At each step, the discarded mass is at most
\tau\cdot Z
. As
\tau\to 0
and
n_{\texttt{max}}\to\infty
, the algorithm converges to the exact decomposition.

Finite termination.

Even when the decomposition is finite, its size can be enormous. Pruning makes the algorithm tractable by bounding the queue at
n_{\texttt{max}}
candidates per step, and also guarantees termination for transducers whose decomposition is infinite (
Section

D.3
).

DeadEnd

Figure 10:
Backtracking. On each retry, halve
\tau
(floored at
\tau_{\min}
) and evict cached decompositions for progressively longer prefixes of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
(depth
D=\min(2^{i-1},D_{\max})
), then recompute
next_dist
. Restore
\tau
afterward.

Backtracking.

Pruning may occasionally lead to dead ends where no valid extension remains. When this occurs while scoring an observed sequence, the next symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
receives zero conditional probability under the approximate
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
. We recover using the procedure in
Figure

10
, which takes the current target string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, the next symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
, the current pruning threshold
\tau
, and hyperparameters: maximum retries
R
, threshold floor
\tau_{\min}
, and maximum eviction depth
D_{\max}
. On each retry,
\tau
is halved (floored at
\tau_{\min}
to prevent unlimited
q
growth), and cached decompositions for prefixes of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
are evicted at exponentially increasing depth
D=\min(2^{i-1},D_{\max})
, forcing
decompose
to recompute with the relaxed threshold. The original threshold is restored afterward.
The procedure is heuristic: there is no guarantee that a fixed number of retries with halved thresholds will recover. Frequent backtracking signals that the pruning threshold is overly aggressive; in such cases, the overhead of repeated eviction and recomputation can dominate runtime.
In our experiments, we use
R=20
,
\tau_{\min}=10^{-10}
,
D_{\max}=32
.
Backtracking is infrequent in practice: for
\tau\leq
1e-3, zero fallbacks occur across all experiments. At the coarsest threshold (
\tau=
1e-1), the mean number of fallbacks per paragraph reaches at most 96 for
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
(max 159), 5.1 for
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
(max 18), and 0.4 for
p_{\mathsf{dna}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{dna2aa}}
(max 5).

C.4
Fast Next-Symbol Decomposition (
decompose_next
)

Since
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\rangle\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})=(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle\cap\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\rangle)\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
. The decomposition for
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
can therefore be obtained with incremental work starting from
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
. The structural reason is monotonicity: the precover for
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
share the same alphabet and initial states; only the accepting states differ, as acceptability depends on which buffers cover the target. This is formalized by the frontier containment property (
Proposition

C.1
).

The
decompose_next
method computes
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
simultaneously, as well as the exact preimage
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
This is the key efficiency improvement over calling
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})

independently for each
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
.

The BFS is seeded with
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(
).
For each source string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
in the queue, we first check whether
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_exact\_member}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(
) and, if so, record
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
in the preimage set.
Next, for each
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}reachable\_outputs}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(defined below;
), we classify
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
as a cylinder or member for
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
; by functionality of
f
, at most one
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
can be a cylinder (
Proposition

C.4
).
If a cylinder was found for some
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
, the entire subtree rooted at
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is absorbed—we skip extension for
all
symbols (
).
Otherwise, we extend by all reachable source symbols (
).

The
decompose_next
algorithm relies on two mechanisms described next: target recursion for cheap frontier computation, and three frontier-based helpers. We now introduce these.

Target recursion.

The call
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_cylinder}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
internally triggers
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
.
Target recursion is used opportunistically when the parent frontier
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
is already cached: the frontier for the extended target is derived by filtering rather than recomputing the entire source-string chain (
Proposition

C.1
). This is the typical case in
decompose_next
, where
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
has already populated the cache.
This optimization is transparent:
is_cylinder
is called unchanged; the logic lives entirely inside
run
.

Per-symbol frontier helpers.

The BFS loop uses three frontier-based helpers.

•

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_exact\_member}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
checks whether
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
maps to
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
exactly, i.e.,
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_exact\_member}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\Longleftrightarrow({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.

•

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}reachable\_inputs}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
conservatively approximates the set of source symbols
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}
such that extending
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}
remains compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, i.e.,
\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\colon\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x^{\prime}}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\neq\emptyset\}
. The pseudocode checks for transitions from frontier states whose extended buffer is prefix-compatible with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
; this may over-report when the resulting state cannot reach acceptance.

•

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}reachable\_outputs}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
conservatively approximates the set of target symbols
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}
such that some extension of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
can produce
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}
as an output prefix, i.e.,
\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}\colon\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}})\neq\emptyset\}
. The pseudocode checks single-step transitions from frontier states, which may over-report (a transition from a frontier state that cannot reach acceptance) because the frontier is not cotrimmed. Symbols requiring multiple transitions to extend past
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
are handled by the expansion loop.

All three are computed directly from the frontier
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
:

⬇

Expansion loop.

The following algorithm combines the seeding, shortcuts, and helpers above into a single BFS that computes the per-symbol quotients and remainders for every
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
simultaneously.

Figure 11:
Fast next-token decomposition algorithm.

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose\_next}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
computes
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
in a single BFS pass.
The
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}is\_cylinder}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
call triggers
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
, which is computed cheaply via target recursion (
Proposition

C.1
) since the parent frontier
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
is already cached.
The non-cylinder shortcut avoids the universality check entirely for seeds in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
After the BFS, the cache is populated so that subsequent
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
calls are free.
The exact preimage is collected as a byproduct: if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is a cylinder for some
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
, every extension produces output strictly longer than
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, so no extension can be an exact preimage of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.

Cache population.

After the BFS, the computed
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
should be written into
decompose
’s memoization cache. A subsequent
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
call (e.g., from
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose\_next}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
) hits the cache instead of re-running its own BFS.

⬇

C.5
Next-Symbol Decomposition Shortcuts

The
decompose_next
algorithm (
Figure

11
) shares work across all output symbols by making a single BFS pass for all output symbols at once instead of calling the decomposition for each symbol as in
decompose
(
Figure

2
). By starting with the decomposition from the prior step and using target recursion, the overall frontier computations are reused.
The remaining bottleneck is the per-symbol cylinder check
is_cylinder
(
Figure

9
). Three shortcuts avoid or reduce this cost, each justified by a proposition about the structure of the precovers.

Non-cylinder monotonicity
and
cylinder uniqueness
enable skipping BFS for some settings.
Combined universality
resolves additional symbols by examining how a frontier partitions into committed and boundary elements.

(1) Non-cylinder shortcut.

Strings in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
cannot be quotient elements for
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. And since
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}})\rangle\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle
, they are not quotient elements for the strictly more restrictive
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
either. The expensive cylinder check (
) is thus skipped for these seeds (
).

Proposition C.3

(Non-cylinder monotonicity)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
.

We have that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
implies
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\not\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, and thus
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\not\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
, so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
.∎

(2) Cylinder uniqueness shortcut.

Since the transformations we consider are functions, at most one output symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
can have
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
as a cylinder element. Once a cylinder element is found, the cylinder check is skipped for the remaining output symbols (
).

Proposition C.4

(Cylinder uniqueness)
.

For a function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
and target prefix
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}})\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}})
.
Then
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}^{-1}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}^{-1}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*})=\emptyset
,
where the last equality holds because
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is a function and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}=\emptyset
.
This contradicts
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\neq\emptyset
.
∎

(3) Combined universality shortcut.

Given
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
, we want to determine whether
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}})
using the covering frontier at
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
without running a full powerset BFS.
The frontier
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
partitions into
committed
elements
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
(the accumulated output already extends past
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
with symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
) and
boundary
elements
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
(the next output symbol is undetermined).
The committed states alone may not be universal—some input symbols may lack outgoing transitions from committed states. Boundary states can fill the gaps, provided every transition from a boundary state produces
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
as its next output symbol.

Proposition C.5

(Combined universality)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
, and define

\displaystyle S_{c}

\displaystyle=\{s\mid(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}),\,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}\},

\displaystyle S_{b}

\displaystyle=\{s\mid(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}),\,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\}

If the union
S_{c}\cup S_{b}
is universal (i.e.,
\llbracket\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[S_{c}\cup S_{b}]})\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
),
S_{c}\cap\mathsf{F}\neq\emptyset
, and every transition from a boundary state produces
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
, except that
\varepsilon
-input transitions may produce
\varepsilon
(i.e.,
s\in S_{b}
and
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}
implies
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
or
({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}=\varepsilon\land{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}=\varepsilon)
), then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}})
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
be arbitrary.
By universality of
S_{c}\cup S_{b}
, there exists
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
with
s\in S_{c}\cup S_{b}
such that
s
has an accepting run on
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
in the input projection.
If
s\in S_{c}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
, so the output of the corresponding path in
f
already begins with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
, giving
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}})
.
If
s\in S_{b}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. By the exclusivity hypothesis, every non-
\varepsilon
-input transition from
s
produces output
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
, and
\varepsilon
-input transitions produce
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
or
\varepsilon
. States reachable from
s
via
\varepsilon{:}\varepsilon
arcs are also in
S_{b}
(since the frontier is
\varepsilon
-closed and the buffer is unchanged), so they satisfy the same constraint. Therefore the first non-
\varepsilon
output along any path from
s
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
, and the output begins with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}
, giving
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}})
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
was arbitrary,
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}})
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}})
.
∎

This shortcut is complementary to the IP-universality shortcut (
Proposition

C.6
): IP-universality is a per-state property, while combined universality is a set-level property that leverages partial coverage from both committed and boundary states.

The proposition requires set-level universality of
S_{c}\cup S_{b}
(the input-projection NFA started from those states accepts
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
). In practice, we use a stronger but cheaper sufficient condition: we require every individual state in
S_{c}\cup S_{b}
to be in the precomputed
\mathsf{U}
. This runs in
O(|\mathcal{F}|)
time, whereas the set-level check requires a powerset BFS that may be as expensive as the full
is_cylinder
check this shortcut is meant to avoid.

⬇

C.6
Input-Projection Universality Shortcut (
fast_univ_filter
)

Recall that the cylinder check
is_cylinder
verifies whether every extension of a source prefix maps through the target prefix, using a powerset BFS that can be expensive.
A cheap sufficient condition avoids it in many cases: a state
s\in\mathsf{S}
is
input-projection universal
if the input projection of the transducer started from
s
accepts all of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
—intuitively, no matter what input follows, the transducer can still produce output.
Let
\mathsf{U}\subseteq\mathsf{S}
be the set of all input-projection universal states.

Proposition C.6

(IP-universality shortcut)
.

If some covering frontier state of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
is IP-universal, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is a cylinder:

\bigl(\exists\,(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\land s\in\mathsf{U}\bigr)\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.

Let
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})\in\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
and
s\in\mathsf{U}
be the witness from the antecedent.
Then
\llbracket\mathsf{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]})\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, so for any
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, there exists an accepting run of
f
on
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
passing through
s
with output prefix
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
Hence
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
was arbitrary,
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
∎

Figure

12
shows how to efficiently precompute the set of input-projection universal states.
We first input-project the transducer and remove
\varepsilon
-transitions (replacing each path
s\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\cdots\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}}s^{\prime\prime}
with a direct transition
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}}s^{\prime\prime}
; see, e.g.,
Hopcroft et al. 2001
) to obtain an
\varepsilon
-free NFA
A
over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
. Both operations preserve the state set: input projection drops output labels, and
\varepsilon
-removal modifies only transitions (adding shortcuts through
\varepsilon
-closures), so
\mathsf{S}_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{A}}=\mathsf{S}
and states in
\mathsf{U}
correspond directly to transducer states in the frontier. A state
s
of
A
is input-projection universal iff it is accepting and, for every
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
, has at least one
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
-successor that is also input-projection universal—a greatest-fixpoint condition. We compute the fixpoint in linear time using an algorithm analogous to Kahn’s algorithm for topological sorting
(
Kahn 1962
)
: initialize the candidate set
\mathsf{U}
to the accepting states of
A
and maintain per-state, per-symbol successor counts. When a count reaches zero, remove the state and propagate the change to its predecessors via a reverse transition index. Each transition is processed at most once, yielding
\mathcal{O}(|\mathsf{T}_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{A}}|)
time.

For transducers in which most accepting states are input-projection universal, this shortcut avoids the universality BFS in

for the vast majority of cylinder checks. In BPE, where all states are input-projection universal, the universality BFS is never needed.

C.7
Memoization and Batching of
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}

The source LM is queried in two places: first during decomposition, where probability-mass pruning calls
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
to rank candidates, and then during scoring, where
prefix_prob
and
next_dist
sum
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
and
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
over the quotient and remainder.
These calls involve many overlapping source prefixes.
Naïvely, each call recomputes the entire chain
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1})\cdot{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{2}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1})\cdots
from scratch.
Memoizing the conditional
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
avoids this: given a cached state for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
, extending by one symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
requires a single conditional evaluation rather than replaying the entire history.

For transformer language models, the cached state is the
key–value (KV) cache
: the accumulated key and value tensors from the self-attention layers.
Extending by one token reuses the cached KV entries and runs a single forward pass for the new position.
Moreover, most LMs return the complete next-symbol distribution
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
in a single call, so we cache the full distribution rather than individual values.
When the extension loop generates children
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{k}
, their prefix probabilities are read directly from the cached distribution without additional LM calls.
27
27

27

Our implementation uses
genlm
(
https://github.com/genlm/genlm-backend
) for its seamless caching and auto-batching interface.

Batching while pruning.

The
prune
step (
Section

C.3
) needs
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
for every string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
in the queue.
Many of these strings share a common prefix but extend by different symbols.
We group the queue by parent: for each parent
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<N}
whose next-symbol distribution
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<N})
is not yet cached, we issue a single batched LM call.
Each call returns the full distribution, from which
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<N}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})={\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<N})\cdot{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{<N})
is read off for all children simultaneously.
On GPU-based LMs, batching these parent contexts into a single forward pass amortizes the per-call overhead.

C.8
Realizing the Autoregressive Interface

Given the decomposition, the transduced LM interface from
Section

C.1
is implemented as follows.
The prefix probability
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
sums LM prefix probabilities over
Q
and LM string probabilities over
R
.
The string probability
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
sums over the exact preimage.
The next-symbol distribution
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is computed via
decompose_next
, which provides the per-symbol decompositions and exact preimage in a single BFS pass; the computational cost is dominated by
decompose_next
, and once the per-symbol quotients, remainders, and preimage are available, the scoring reduces to lookups into the memoized source LM (
Section

C.7
).

Figure 13:
Fast implementation of the autoregressive interface using
decompose_next
.

For reference, the naïve next-symbol distribution computes
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}prefix\_prob}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
independently for each
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}
(see
Section

C.1
).
The reference implementation is correct, but inefficient because it does not share work: each
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}prefix\_prob}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y^{\prime}})
call triggers its own
decompose
BFS, and it does not benefit from the non-cylinder shortcut, target recursion, or cache population described in
Section

C.4
.

C.9
All-Universal Fast Path

When every transducer state is IP-universal, the remainder is always empty and every newly discovered element is immediately a quotient element, so decomposition terminates in a single BFS round. If, additionally, every non-
\varepsilon
-input arc produces at least one output symbol, then boundary frontier elements can be resolved in closed form via a precomputed first-output table. Together, these two conditions let
next_dist
(
Figure

13
) simplify into
all_universal_next_dist
(
Figure

14
). We describe the simplifications below (normalization is unchanged).

Decompose (simplified).

Because every state is IP-universal,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\emptyset
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
: every source prefix in the precover is in the quotient. The call to
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
(
Figure

2
) terminates in a single BFS round—every newly discovered element is immediately classified as a quotient element. This makes
decompose_next
unnecessary: the per-symbol classification is absorbed into the scoring below.

Score (simplified).

For each
(\mathcal{F},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}
, the frontier partitions into
committed
elements
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}})
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}\succ{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
and
boundary
elements with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, as in the combined universality shortcut (
Section

C.5
). Committed elements have already determined their next output symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}_{N+1}
; because all states are IP-universal, each contributes
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
directly to
\overline{p}[{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\widehat{y}}]
; thus, no expansion is needed.

Boundary elements (
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{b}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
) need one more source symbol to commit to an output.
The no-
\varepsilon
-output precondition ensures that each non-
\varepsilon
-input arc produces at least one output symbol, so a precomputed
first-output table

\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.25,0}fo}}}}(S,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})
(defined in the pseudocode) maps each input symbol to the unique output it produces from a given powerstate. This symbol is unique: functionality of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
requires that all transitions on
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
from states in
S
produce the same output, since IP-universality guarantees each successor has an accepting continuation (so both outputs would be realized). Each boundary element is thus resolved with a single batched LM call:
\ell={\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
is queried once per quotient element, and for each
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
, the mass
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\cdot\ell({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})
is attributed to
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.25,0}fo}}}}(S,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x})
. If any boundary state is accepting, the LM’s
eos
probability contributes
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\cdot\ell(\textsc{eos})
to
\overline{p}[\textsc{eos}]
.

⬇
