Figure 14:
all_universal_next_dist
: specialization of
next_dist
(
Figure

13
) when all states are IP-universal and every non-
\varepsilon
-input arc produces output.
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}=\emptyset
always, so only
Q
contributes. Boundary elements are resolved via
fo
, requiring one LM call per quotient element.

The
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
transducers satisfy both preconditions (
Table

4
: all states IP-universal, no
\varepsilon
-output on non-
\varepsilon
-input arcs), explaining their substantially higher throughput (
Section

7
). The DNA transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{dna2aa}}
has all states IP-universal but uses
\varepsilon
-output arcs (the first two bases of each codon emit no amino acid), so the fast path does not apply.

Appendix D
Proofs for Finiteness Conditions

D.1
Properties of Prefix Monotone Maps

See
6.1

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
: Prefix monotonicity means that for any
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, if we have that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
then
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}})
and thus that there exists a
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}){\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. And since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
was chosen arbitrarily
(2)
holds.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
: Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle)\subseteq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle
. Then,

\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle))\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})).

Note that, for any
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}))\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
.
Therefore,
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}))\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
and so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
. This shows that

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
.

(3)\Rightarrow(4)
: By assumption, any element in the precover is an extension of a quotient element, and thus a member in the cylinder over quotient elements.

(4)\Rightarrow(1)
:
Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is prefix-continuous, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is cylindrical for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
. By prefix-continuity,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
is cylindrical, so
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
. In particular,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}))
, which gives
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}})
.
∎

D.2
Quotient Bound for Strict-Prefix Monotone Maps

The following proposition bounds the size of the quotient when the map is strict-prefix monotone.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
be a strict-prefix monotone map. Then, for every
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\setminus\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})

2.

\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\left({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\sqcup\{\varepsilon\}\right)

3.

\left|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\right|\leq\left(|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}|+1\right)^{|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|}

(1) Observe that (using
6.1
) for every
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})

(11)

In particular, the minimality of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
implies that

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\cap\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\cap\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\rangle

(12)

Consequently, we obtain the identity
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\setminus\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle\setminus\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\rangle
, which in turn yields the inclusion

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\setminus\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\setminus\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})\rangle\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})

(13)

For the reverse inclusion, let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
. Then there exists a unique
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{q}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{q}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}
, by definition of the quotient set.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
is strict-prefix monotone and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{q})\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, we must have
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{q}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}
.
This shows that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, and completes the proof of (1).

(2)
Fix
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
and let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})
.
There exists a unique
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
.
Intersecting
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle
with both sides of (
11
), one obtains

\displaystyle\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle

\displaystyle=(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}))\sqcup\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}^{\prime}))

(14)

\displaystyle=(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}))\sqcup\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\sqcup C_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}

(15)

where
C_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{C}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}^{\prime}))\setminus\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle
.
Note that this set is cylindrical.
By strict monotonicity we have
|\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})|\leq 1
.
Hence either:

(i)
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\emptyset
, in which case

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}),

or

(ii)
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\rangle\cap{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}f^{-1}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\}
.
Then there exists a word
z\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}z
.
Let
z^{\prime}\prec z
. If
z^{\prime}\neq\varepsilon
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}z^{\prime}\in C_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
.
Since
C_{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}
is cylindrical, it contains
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}z^{\prime}\rangle
and in particular it must contain
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle
contradicting the definition of
C_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}}
.
Therefore no non-empty proper prefix
z^{\prime}\prec z
exists, so
z
is a single symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}.

(3) For any
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
and any
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
, we have from (2),

\displaystyle|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y})|\leq(|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}|+1)|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})|

(16)

Iterating this bound along any string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
, we have:

|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})|\leq(|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}|+1)^{|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|}|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}(\varepsilon)|=(|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}|+1)^{|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}|}\qed

D.3
Sufficient Conditions for Finite Quotients and Remainders

6.1
below gives sufficient conditions for when one can derive a finite precover decomposition. An example of such a transducer is given in
3
.

See
6.1

Fix
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}^{*}
; we show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
are finite.
Let
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
be the set of all paths in
f
that emit exactly
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, i.e.,

\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{s_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}}s_{1}\cdots s_{N-1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}_{N}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}}s_{N}\mid s_{0}\in\mathsf{I},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}_{N}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}\}
.
By condition (i), the
\varepsilon
-output subgraph of
f
is acyclic, so any sub-path that emits no output has length at most
|\mathsf{S}|
. Since the total output is
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
, each path has bounded length, and
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
is finite.
Let
\Pi_{\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
be the set of all valid paths formed by extending the roots in
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
until they reach a state satisfying a safety base case.
By condition (ii), every state of
f
is safe—in particular, the end states of paths in
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
. This implies that no extension path can continue indefinitely without satisfying a base case (IP-universality or finite closure).
Since a finite-state transducer has finite branching and no infinite valid extension paths, Kőnig’s Lemma implies that the tree of extensions is finite.
28
28

28

Kőnig’s Lemma states that every infinite tree with finite branching must have an infinite path. Since our alphabet is finite (finite branching) and the Safety condition forbids cycles among non-base-case states (no infinite paths), the resulting tree of extensions must be finite.

Thus,
\Pi_{\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
is a finite set.

Let
\mathsf{S}_{U}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{s\in\mathsf{S}\colon\text{$s$ is IP-universal}\}
and
\mathsf{S}_{C}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\{s\in\mathsf{S}\colon|\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}_{[s]}\rrbracket|<\infty\}
be the sets of states satisfying conditions (a) and (b), respectively, and let
\mathsf{S}_{S}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}\mathsf{S}_{U}\cup\mathsf{S}_{C}
. We can decompose the set of extended paths disjointly based on their end state:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\rho\in\Pi_{\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}\colon\rho_{|\rho|}\in\mathsf{S}_{U}\right\}\sqcup\left\{\rho\in\Pi_{\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}\colon\rho_{|\rho|}\in\mathsf{S}_{C}\right\}

(17)

\displaystyle=\left\{\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot s_{|\boldsymbol{\pi}|}\xrightarrow{\mdots@}s_{N}\mid\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}},s_{N}\in\mathsf{S}_{U},\{s_{|\boldsymbol{\pi}|},\mdots@,s_{N-1}\}\cap\mathsf{S}_{S}=\emptyset\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup\left\{\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot s_{|\boldsymbol{\pi}|}\xrightarrow{\mdots@}s_{N}\mid\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}},s_{N}\in\mathsf{S}_{C},\{s_{|\boldsymbol{\pi}|},\mdots@,s_{N-1}\}\cap\mathsf{S}_{S}=\emptyset\right\}

(18)

The first term corresponds to
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
by definition. Since
\Pi_{\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}}
is finite, this set is finite.
The second term collects paths leading to states
s\in\mathsf{S}_{C}
. The remainder
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
consists of the input strings from these paths, concatenated with the finite language accepted by
s
. Since the number of paths is finite and the closure of each stopping state is finite,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
is finite.
∎

Example 3
.

Consider the transducer below over

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{b}}\}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{d}}\}
,
encoding a (partial) function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
.
The four states illustrate all cases of safety.
State
s_{3}
is universal: it is accepting and loops on every input
symbol with
\varepsilon
-output, so

L(s_{3})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
(base case a).
State
s_{2}
has finite closure: it is accepting with no outgoing
transitions, so
L(s_{2})=\{\varepsilon\}
(base case b).
State
s_{1}
is neither universal nor finite closure, but is safe by the
recursive step (c): its successors
s_{3}
(via
a
) and

s_{2}
(via
b
) are both safe.
Similarly,
s_{0}
is safe because its successors
s_{1}

(via
a
) and
s_{2}
(via
b
) are both safe.

For target
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}
, the path set

\Pi\smash{{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}}}=\{s_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}:{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}}s_{1}\}

is finite (condition i), with terminal state
s_{1}
safe (condition ii).
Extending from
s_{1}
: input
a
reaches the universal
state
s_{3}
, contributing
aa
to

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}})
; input
b
reaches the finite-closure state
s_{2}
, contributing
ab
to

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}})
.
The root string
a
is also in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}})
:

s_{1}
is accepting with
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}}
, but
a
is not cylindrical since

ab
cannot be further extended (stuck at
s_{2}
).
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aa}}\}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{a}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ab}}\}
.

Appendix E
Example Decompositions

We give three concrete examples of decompositions and prefix probabilities.

Example 4
.

``
) into a quotation mark (

) but otherwise keeps the symbols

and

fixed. It can be seen as a minimal example of text normalization.

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.11113pt}{31.93068pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}\right]

(19a)

When computing the prefix probability
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\textasciigrave}})
,
sequences starting with two backticks get mapped to a quote and thus do not appear in the precover. Instead, a single backtick remains in the remainder, while a backtick followed by
a
goes into the quotient, as any further extensions preserve the initial backtick. We thus have (by visually canceling out continuations that get mapped to quotation marks),

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\textasciigrave}})

\displaystyle=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}a}}},{\ldots}\right\}\cup\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}a}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}a{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}aa}},{\ldots}\right\}

(19b)

\displaystyle=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}\right\}\sqcup\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}\right\}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}

(19c)

Thus,
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\textasciigrave}})=p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}})+{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}a}})
.
Similarly, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{"}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\textasciigrave}}}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
and thus
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{"}})={\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{\textasciigrave}})
.

4
demonstrates the efficiency of using the remainder and the quotient; both sets only have a single element, yet they fully specify what probabilities contribute to
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\textasciigrave}})
. There are, however, edge cases that do not lend themselves to such efficient decompositions.

Example 5
.

Consider the following mapping
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
and its representation as a transducer
f
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}^{n})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}}^{n}\textbf{ if }n\text{ is even}\textbf{ else }{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}^{n}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}=\left[\noindent\par\noindent\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\ignorespaces\hbox to136.66pt{\vbox to100.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.69774pt\lower-70.50554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}}}\hskip 11.38092pt\right]

(20a)

with
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}\}
.
The precover for
b
is given by

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})=\left\{\cancel{\varepsilon},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aa}},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaa}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaaa}},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaaaa}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaaaaa}},\ldots\right\}

(20b)

This example is, unfortunately, challenging for our approach as the remainder set is not finite:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}^{2\,n}\mid n>0\right\},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})=\emptyset

(20c)

In other words, the mapping never releases the evenness constraint, and we sum forever.

Finally, we give an example of a simple transducer with a single remainder element in
6
.

Example 6
.

Consider the following mapping
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}
, visualized in
Figure

15
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}f}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}^{n})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}}^{n}\textbf{ if }n\neq 2\textbf{ else }{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}

(21a)

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}\}
.
Suppose we want to compute the prefix probability
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}},\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aa}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaa}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaaa}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaaaa}},\ldots\right\}

(21b)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}\right\},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaa}}\right\}

(21c)

Thus,

\displaystyle{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{b}})}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})

(21d)

\displaystyle=p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}})+{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{aaa}})

(21e)

Figure 15
:
An FST that maps
n
occurrences of ’a’ to the same number of ’b’s, except when the input is exactly two ’a’s, which are mapped to ’c’.

Figure 16:
An FST where the
\varepsilon
-output arc
q_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}:\varepsilon}q_{1}
creates a frontier element whose output has not yet reached the target. For target
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}}
and source prefix
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}}
, the frontier is
\mathcal{F}=\{(q_{1},\varepsilon),(q_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}})\}
. The covering states
\{q_{2}\}
are not input-projection universal (
q_{2}
has no arc on
b
), so
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}fast\_univ\_filter}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}})
fails and
a
would be misclassified as non-quotient. However,
q_{1}
handles input
b
and produces
c
, matching the target. The BFS in
is_cylinder
confirms universality over the full frontier, correctly placing
a
in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{c}})
.

Appendix F
Experimental Setup

Here we detail the experimental setup for reproducing the experiments in
Section

7
and
Section

G.5
.

F.1
Datasets

For the tokens-to-byte and PTB experiments in
Section

7
, we choose the first 10 paragraphs (excluding headers) of the test split in the
wikitext-2-raw-v1
dataset
(
Merity et al. 2017
)
(corresponding to the first 7684 bytes) from the
Hugging Face datasets library. We use the first 256 bytes of the same dataset and split to run the experiments in
Section

G.5
.
For the DNA experiments in
Section

7
, we sample 65 human proteins
29
29

29

https://www.uniprot.org/uniprotkb?query=Human
, each consisting of 4-12 amino acids, with their accession numbers given in
Table

1
.

F.2
Models

We conduct experiments using GPT-2 Large
(
Radford et al. 2019
)
, Llama 3.2-1B, Llama 3.1-8B and Phi-4
(
Abdin et al. 2024
, 14B;)
30
30

30

Available under
openai/gpt2-large
,
meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B
,
meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B
and
microsoft/phi-4
at
https://huggingface.co
.
from the
Hugging Face hub
(
Wolf et al. 2020
)
. We use the
library
31
31

31

https://github.com/genlm/genlm-backend
and the

(
Kwon et al. 2023
)
backend to efficiently evaluate the models.

For the PTB experiments we use
32
32

32

https://github.com/genlm/genlm-bytes
to convert token-level models into byte-level models and compose them with
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
. For
we use a beam size of
K
=5 and a pruning threshold of 0.001. For the DNA experiments we train a custom GPT-2 Small model
33
33

33

https://huggingface.co/vesteinn/gpt2-dna
on a human DNA dataset
34
34

34

https://huggingface.co/datasets/simecek/Human_DNA_v0
.
. The token set of the model is
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{G}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{T}}\right\}
, eliminating the need for composing the model with a transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
that maps from subword tokens to bytes. For training parameters, see
Section

F.4
, for training and validation metrics, see
Section

F.4
.

F.3
Parameters

For all experiments, we use the pruning heuristic described in
Section

C.3
. For the experiments in
Section

7
we report the results for different values of
n_{\texttt{max}}\in\{5000,10000,15000,20000,25000,30000\}
. For all other experiments, we set
n_{\texttt{max}}=\infty
.

F.4
GPU Usage

All experiments in
Section

7
use a single NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 (24 GB), except Phi-4, which requires two. The benchmarks in
Section

G.5
use an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090.

Table 2:
Training parameters for the GPT-2 small model, trained on a human DNA.

Parameter

Value

Learning Rate

0.0003

Optimizer

AdamW

\beta_{1}=0.9
,
\beta_{2}=0.999
,

\epsilon=1\mathrm{e}{-8}

Learning Rate Scheduler

Linear

Warm-up Steps

Constructing the PTB transducer.

We construct the PTB FST by encoding each tokenizer rule
35
35

35

See
https://www.nltk.org/_modules/nltk/tokenize/treebank.html#TreebankWordTokenizer
for the full specification.
as an FST that segments character sequences by inserting a distinguished separator symbol
sep

\notin{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}
. Specifically, the transducer inserts
sep
at punctuation and clitic boundaries, and collapses whitespace characters (spaces, tabs, newlines, etc.) into
sep
. Note that the resulting transduced language model is thus not a true distribution over PTB tokens, but over characters and separators corresponding to the same boundaries that the PTB tokens would have. This is a pragmatic decision, as the PTB tokenizer can tokenize any sentence into orthographic words. In other words, it would accept an infinite vocabulary. Such a transducer can be built on the fly and would be equivalent to one with infinitely many states. In this paper, we only use the finite version. An example of such a rule is given in
Figure

5
, which inserts
sep
before a comma if it is not followed by a digit. We then compose these FSTs into a single transducer (
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
). Note that context-dependent rules, such as the one given in
Figure

5
, introduce non-IP-universal states. For example, state
q_{2}
only accepts
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{{0--9,}}}\}
.
In fact, of the 479 states in
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
, just 56 are IP-universal.

Section

7
, is partially shown in
Figure

17
.

Appendix G
Additional Experimental Results

Here we provide complementary results to the experiments presented in
Section

7
, using the algorithms described in
Appendix

C
. First, in
Section

G.1
we compare our transducer-based method using
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
to

(
Vieira et al. 2025a
)
, then give full JSD (
Section

G.2
) comparisons for
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{dna2aa}}
. In
Section

G.4
we report cross-entropy comparisons for
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
, and finally ablate the importance of IP-universal states (
Section

G.5
).

G.1
Comparison to
Vieira et al. 2025a

Vieira et al. 2025a
convert token-level models to byte-level models using a beam-search algorithm parameterized by a beam size
K
. We compare our transducer-based approach with their method by computing the JSD between the two resulting distributions over the paragraphs from WikiText (
Appendix

F
).
Table

6
uses genlm-bytes (
K\!=\!32
, pruning threshold of 0.0) as a reference and varies our pruning threshold
\tau
. As
\tau
decreases, JSD drops, and our distributions converge to the baseline. At moderate thresholds (
\tau\leq
1e-3), JSD is already below 1.5e-3 for all models, confirming that the two methods produce closely matching distributions.

Table 5:

\tau=0
) vs. genlm-bytes (exact) for

To verify that the two methods compute the same underlying distribution in the exact setting, we compare them with no pruning:
\tau=0
(no pruning) for our method and exact inference for genlm-bytes (
K=\infty
).
Table

5
shows the per-position JSD and maximum absolute difference for the first 10 bytes of the WikiText evaluation data using
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
. The JSD values are on the order of 1e-14–1e-15, and the maximum absolute probability difference is at most 3.4e-6, confirming that both methods give the same distribution within floating-point precision.

Table

7
reports the JSD and throughput of the baseline itself at
K\in\{8,16,32\}
, showing the accuracy–speed trade-off within their method. Reducing K from 32 to 8 substantially increases throughput while introducing only modest changes in JSD, indicating that the baseline’s approximation error at
K\!=\!32
is small. Note that the method of
Vieira et al. 2025a
is designed specifically for strictly prefix monotone transformations, a property that
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
satisfies. This specialization enables a trie-based algorithm that achieves a better accuracy–speed trade-off than our more general-purpose approach on this class of transformations.

G.2
Jensen–Shannon Divergence

We benchmark how well the algorithm given in
Appendix

C
approximates the exact distribution when using high pruning thresholds
\tau
in the probability mass pruning described in
Section

C.3
. Across all three settings—token-to-byte, PTB tokenization, and DNA-to-amino-acid—JSD decreases as
\tau
decreases, at the cost of throughput (bytes/sec).

Table

8
reports the token-to-byte results (
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
) for all four models. JSD drops by two to three orders of magnitude from
\tau=
1e-1 to
\tau=
3e-5, with the tightest thresholds reaching JSD below 1e-4 for all models.

Table

9
gives the corresponding results for PTB tokenization (
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
), complementing
Figure

6
(right). Although
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
has fewer states than the token-to-byte transducers, only 56 of its 479 states are IP-universal, making lower thresholds computationally expensive; the reference is therefore
\tau=
1e-4 (3e-4 for
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}
) rather than 1e-5. Despite this, JSD converges quickly, reaching the order of 1e-5 at
\tau=
3e-4 for
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}
,
p_{\mathsf{llama1B}}
, and
p_{\mathsf{llama8B}}
, while
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}
converges more slowly.

Table

10
reports the DNA-to-amino-acid results (
p_{\mathsf{dna}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{dna2aa}}
), where we additionally vary the candidate-set cap
n_{\texttt{max}}
to mitigate the combinatorial blow-up inherent in the three-to-one nucleotide-to-amino-acid mapping. Tighter thresholds generally reduce JSD. However, the interaction between
\tau
and
n_{\texttt{max}}
is less uniform, as both the evaluated and reference distributions depend on the cap.

Incomplete runs.

As shown in
Section

G.3
, the decomposition size grows rapidly at tight thresholds—mean
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}|
exceeds 35,000 at
\tau=
1e-5 for
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
. For some model–threshold combinations, this growth prevents calculating the full distribution at each position in all ten evaluation paragraphs. Specifically,
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
in
Table

8
completed only 7 of 10 paragraphs at
\tau=
1e-5. In
Table

9
,
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
completed 7 of 10 paragraphs at its reference threshold of
\tau=
3e-4, and
p_{\mathsf{llama1B}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
completed 9 of 10 at
\tau=
1e-4. Additionally,
p_{\mathsf{phi4}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
completed only 9 of 10 paragraphs at
\tau=
1e-1 due to an unrecoverable backtracking failure (
Figure

10
). In these cases, JSD and throughput are computed over the paragraphs shared across all thresholds for that model–transducer combination.

G.3
Decomposition Size

Table

11
and
Figure

18
show how the quotient and remainder sizes grow as
\tau
decreases, explaining the throughput reduction observed in the JSD experiments.

For the all-IP-universal token-to-byte and DNA transducers, the remainder is empty (
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}|\!=\!0
) at every position, so only the quotient size is reported. In the token-to-byte setting, the mean quotient size grows from 62 at
\tau=
1e-1 to over 35,000 at
\tau=
1e-5, directly accounting for the throughput reduction at tight thresholds. The DNA transducer saturates early due to
n_{\texttt{max}}=5000
: mean
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}|
reaches 465 by
\tau=
3e-5 and remains flat beyond that.

For
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}
, which has non-IP-universal states, both
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}|
and
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}|
are reported.
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}|
grows steadily with a tighter
\tau
, while
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}|
initially decreases as more candidates enter the quotient but then grows as the expansion loop over non-IP-universal states discovers additional remainder elements. The presence of a non-trivial remainder is the key structural difference from the all-IP-universal transducers: each remainder element requires a full string probability
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
rather than just a prefix probability, making it more expensive per element.

|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}|
across sequence positions for three transducers at varying pruning thresholds

. Results are computed on paragraph 1 of WikiText (833 bytes for
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
, 850 bytes for

) and on the longest protein in the test set (P83127, 12 amino acids for

{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}

.

G.4
Cross-Entropy

Cross-entropy measures how well the transduced model assigns probability to held-out text; unlike JSD, it only requires evaluating
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}y}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
for the observed next symbol rather than computing the full next-symbol distribution
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.
Table

12
reports cross-entropy (in nats and bits per byte) together with throughput for (
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
). Because only a single probability is needed per position, throughput is higher than in the JSD tables above. Cross-entropy converges quickly as
\tau
decreases: for all four models, the tightest thresholds yield nearly identical values, confirming that moderate pruning suffices for accurate sequence scoring.

Table 12:
Cross-entropy and throughput for various thresholds
\tau
using
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
. 95
\%
confidence intervals are given in parentheses.
p_{\mathsf{gpt2}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
at
\tau=
1e-5 is computed over 6 paragraphs; all other configurations use 10.

G.5
Benchmarking the Computational Shortcut

We benchmark the computational shortcut inherent in the prefix decomposition described in
Section

4
, which allows us to decompose the precover into remainder and quotient representatives.
We generate suboptimal decompositions by randomly selecting
n
IP-universal states in the transducer (see
Section

4
) and treating them as non-IP-universal in our algorithms (see
Section

5
). This gradually decreases the size of the quotient and increases the remainder correspondingly.
Table

13
shows the average JSD between the original and modified distributions over the first 256 bytes of the first 5 paragraphs of the
wikitext-2-raw-v1
dataset
(
Merity et al. 2017
)
. For each value of
n
, we repeat the sampling of new non-universal states three times, and report the mean JSD.

The distributions diverge rapidly, and after converting roughly 15–20% of IP-universal states, the algorithm repeatedly encounters dead ends. This number varies between runs; as shown in
Figure

19
, each repeat exhibits a distinct step function: JSD remains low until a particular high-connectivity state is converted, then jumps by 2–3 orders of magnitude and plateaus. In the PTB transducer, one IP-universal state (state 193) serves as a routing hub with 274 arcs spanning 245 output symbols. When this state is converted, frontiers containing it move from the quotient
Q
to the remainder
R
, causing the expansion loop to require fallback scoring (
Figure

10
). Different runs encounter this hub at different values depending on the permutation order, producing the staircase pattern. This shows that IP-universal states have a hierarchical importance structure: distribution quality is governed by a small number of high-connectivity hub states, while the majority can be converted with negligible impact (
\text{JSD}\leq
1e-4).

).

Appendix H
State-Based Decomposition

The algorithms presented in the main text enumerate source
strings
: the quotient
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and remainder
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
are represented as explicit sets of strings.
When these sets are infinite, the string-enumeration approach does not terminate without pruning.

An alternative is to operate on the
state space
of the precover DFA rather than its string space.
Since the precover DFA
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
(
Section

5.1
) has finitely many states, a state-based traversal always terminates, even when
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
are infinite.
The key idea is to represent the quotient and remainder as DFAs
Q
and
R
—automata that
accept
the (possibly infinite) sets
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
respectively—rather than enumerating their elements.
The full precover is then recovered as
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})=\langle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rrbracket\rangle\sqcup\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}\rrbracket
.

The algorithm in
Figure

20
performs a BFS over the states of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.
At each state
s
, it checks whether
s
is
universal
—i.e., whether the sub-automaton rooted at
s
accepts
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}^{*}
(see
is_cylinder
).
If so,
s
is marked as a quotient member and accepting state, and its successors are not explored, since all extensions from a universal state are covered.
If
s
is accepting but not universal, it is marked as a remainder member and accepting state, and exploration continues through its outgoing arcs.

The result is a pair of DFAs that share the same state space and truncated arc set but differ in their accepting states.
The quotient automaton
Q
has the universal states as accepting states, and arcs leaving universal states are dropped (since the BFS does not expand past them); thus
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{Q}}\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, accepting exactly the quotient elements.
The remainder automaton
R
uses the same truncated arc set but marks only the non-universal accepting states; thus,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{R}}\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
.

Figure 20:
State-based decomposition of the precover into DFAs.
Left
: BFS over the precover DFA
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
. Universal states become accepting in
Q
; non-universal accepting states become accepting in
R
. Arcs leaving universal states are not collected.
Right
:
is_cylinder
is the same universality BFS as in
Figure

3
, but takes a DFA state directly rather than computing it from a source string via
\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}run}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
.

Comparison with string-based algorithms.

The state-based algorithm always terminates in finite time—even when
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
are infinite—because
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}}
has finitely many states.
The resulting DFAs
Q
and
R
provide compact, finite representations of these potentially infinite sets.

However, implementing the autoregressive interface (
Section

C.1
) still requires enumerating the strings accepted by these machines: computing
{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\mathcal{Y}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
via
Equation

8a
sums
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{x}})
over elements of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{Q}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{y}})
, and when these sets are infinite, the sum must be truncated regardless.
Thus, while the DFA representation guarantees a finite decomposition, it does not, on its own, yield finite-time scoring.

Appendix I
Related Work

Modern language models define probability distributions over sequences of tokens (see
Section

2
). For efficiency and vocabulary (a.k.a. their alphabet) management, they usually rely on subword schemes such as BPE
(
Sennrich et al. 2016
;
Gage 1994
)
or Unigram
(
Kudo 2018
)
. Although these approaches have been remarkably successful, their units often don’t coincide with linguistic boundaries, and any given string typically admits an exponential number of tokenization variants with non-zero probability mass under the language model. Recent work has tackled this issue by enforcing canonical tokenization to remove probability mass from noncanonical encodings
(
Vieira et al. 2025b
)
, while
Geh et al. 2024
have shown that aggregating the probability mass of noncanonical tokenization choices carries a useful signal that can boost downstream accuracy.

Subword segmentation also gives rise to the prompt-boundary problem
(
Vieira et al. 2025a
)
, where imperceptible changes to the final characters of a prompt (e.g., appending a single whitespace) can push the encoded token sequence onto a completely different path in token space, causing the model to abandon otherwise highly probable continuations. To overcome these issues,
Vieira et al. 2025a
introduce an algorithm for transforming token-based language models into language models over characters. Although their contribution centers around characters, the underlying idea can be generalized (as shown in this work).

Many applications need a method to accurately convert the probability mass learned on subword tokens to other types of units, such as bytes, words, or morphemes in NLP, or amino acids in computational biology, as pointed out in
Section

1
.
An additional example is found in psycholinguistics, where researchers often require fine-grained estimates of surprisal, e.g., to predict a reader’s likelihood of skipping a word based on how predictable its first three characters are
(
Rayner et al. 1982
;
Blanchard et al. 1989
)
.
To this end, recent studies have tackled the challenges posed by subword tokenization
(
Nair & Resnik 2023
;
Beinborn & Pinter 2023
;
Pimentel & Meister 2024
;
Oh & Schuler 2024
;
Giulianelli et al. 2024
)
. For example,
Oh & Schuler 2024
and
Pimentel & Meister 2024
argue that leading whitespace tokenization introduces a confounder in surprisal estimates and instead advocate for incorporating the probability of trailing whitespaces into such calculations.

Furthermore,
Pimentel & Meister 2024
give a bespoke procedure for converting token-based language models to word-based language models.
However, their method does not model the contextually sensitive nature of English word segmentation, e.g., it treats both periods in
Section

1
identically, where English orthography does not.
Additionally, the justification of the procedure requires that there exists a set of distinguished end-of-word markers that appear at the end of a token, if at all. We now consider how such a transducer can be constructed.
Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}
be a transducer that converts a token alphabet to a character alphabet, and
D
be the set of delimiters. The transducer
{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{D}
is given in
Figure

21
. Given a language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}
, we can then compose them into a transducer
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{\alpha}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{f}}}_{D}
to get a transduced language model over separator-delimited words. However, such an approach would be rather naïve. Unfortunately, delimiter-based separation would not be able to distinguish when the dot should be its own symbol or not, as in
Section

1
.

q_{0}
q_{1}
d
:
d
,
\forall d\in D

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
:␣
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
,
\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\setminus D

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}
,
\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{X}}\setminus D

d

,
\forall d\in D

Figure 21:
A simple FST that segments character streams into words without contextual information, inserting ␣ at the start of each word.

The delimiter-based approach also fails for most BPE-based language models because of the clustering of delimiter candidates.
For instance, GPT-4o’s alphabet contains the token
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{!!!}}}}}{{\text{\raisebox{-2.15277pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{10880}}}}}}
, which consists solely of end-of-word symbols.
Under PTB guidelines, for example,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{!!!}}}}}{{\text{\raisebox{-2.15277pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{10880}}}}}}
should be broken into three consecutive orthographic words
!

!

!
.
In contrast, we argue that the proper tokenization scheme for psycholinguistic modeling should be specified based on the goals of the study and not based on the properties of any one specific tokenizer.

Tokenization challenges are not unique to modeling natural language. In computational biology, DNA, RNA, and protein sequences are long, unsegmented strings over small alphabets that pose challenges in tokenization. Researchers thus alternate between different tokenization schemas, such as k-mers, learned subwords, and motif-aware segmenters
(
Ji et al. 2021
;
Nguyen et al. 2023
;
Dotan et al. 2024
;
Wang et al. 2024
;
Qiao et al. 2024
)
. Because language models are often trained under different tokenization schemas, their autoregressive predictions—step-by-step probabilities and conditional distributions—are not directly comparable across tokenization schemes.

Transducer-based approaches to tokenization are well-established—WordPiece
(
Wu et al. 2016
)
can be implemented as a transducer
(
Song et al. 2021
)
, and deterministic finite automata have been constructed for BPE
(
Berglund & van der Merwe 2023
;
Berglund et al. 2024
)
. Moreover, transducers also have a long history in language modeling
(
Mohri 1997
)
and have been adopted for constrained decoding, where an FST enforces lexical or structural constraints
(
Allauzen et al. 2014
;
Ghazvininejad et al. 2016
;
Stahlberg et al. 2019
;
Willard & Louf 2023
;
Koo et al. 2024
;
Cognetta et al. 2025
)
.

Closely related are neural finite-state transducers
(
Lin et al. 2019
)
and earlier neural FST hybrids
(
Rastogi et al. 2016
)
, which assign (neural) weights to transitions and compute prefix/sequence probabilities as path-sums over all paths consistent with a given output. Our construction admits a similar view: we marginalize over all source strings (paths) that map to a given output. However, rather than learning transition weights, we focus on transducing an off-the-shelf pretrained LM, enabling efficient inference via quotient-remainder decomposition.

In this study, we generalize character-level conversion and extend
Vieira et al. 2025a
into a framework that enables transforming a language model into another language model, beyond the limited setting of strict-prefix monotonic mappings. We support conversions between sets of units and unit-preserving transformations, provided that the mapping between them can be described by a finite-state transducer.

Appendix J
Limitations

Empirical scope.

Although our framework theoretically enables transduction of any language model to any unit of interest, given a valid transducer, we test only a limited set of architectures (GPT-2, LLaMA 3, and Phi-4) and target specific units (bytes, Penn Treebank tokens, and amino acids). Future research could broaden the analysis to a wider range of models, datasets, and units.

Expressiveness.

Our analysis has focused on functional finite-state transducers and regular languages. Future work could consider dynamically built transducers and distributions over more expressive languages, as well as stochastic maps encoded by non-functional transducers—where the notion of universality would need to be adjusted.

Approximation quality.

Our pruning-based inference algorithm performs well when the source language model concentrates most of its probability mass on a small number of prefixes that map to the current target prefix under the transducer—i.e., when the effective size of the quotient and remainder after pruning is small. When the mass is dispersed across many source prefixes (such as in the DNA to amino-acid case), pruning must discard a larger fraction of the total probability, and the approximation degrades. An importance-sampling approach (
Section

C.3
) could provide a less systematically biased alternative.

Speed.

The speed of our algorithms and implementations may not suit every use case. Obtaining the full distribution (for example, for decoding or model comparisons) currently requires speeds of around 10–20 bytes/sec. While this is not prohibitive for many tasks, it leaves ample room for improvement.

Marginalization.

A core property of transduced language models is the
marginalization
: the transduction sums source-string probabilities to compute target-string probabilities, aggregating mass across all source strings that map to the same target. Since natural language allows the same meaning to be expressed in multiple ways, a transduction could normalize these to obtain a more representative output distribution over the space of interest. Consider, for instance, a math problem: “If 12 students are younger than Tom and 12 students are older, how many are there in total?” The answer could be ‘25’, or ‘twenty-five’, or perhaps ‘12+1+12’. If any of these have significant probability mass, we would like to sum them. In chain-of-thought reasoning, we can similarly imagine summing and normalizing probabilities across samples from the system, an approach known as
self-consistency

(
Wang et al. 2023
)
. We hope to see transduced language models applied in such settings in future work.
